My supervisor has shown me reports and basically said, “Look, this doesn’t prove anyone is tracking your buys or changing the launch numbers,” and I can look at it and understand what he’s saying. I’m not sitting there unable to read it or anything. I get the explanation.\n\nBut the feeling still stays there. It’s like my mind goes, okay, maybe that report doesn’t show it, or maybe the numbers were changed in a way that wouldn’t be obvious, or maybe the vendors and people at work know how to keep it from looking direct. And I know how that sounds. I know it sounds like I’m moving the goalposts, but that’s what’s scary about it — even when someone gives me something that should calm me down, it doesn’t really calm me down.\n\nSo if it was truly certain, like somehow completely impossible that anyone was messing with things, maybe part of me would try to accept that. But I think another part of me would still be suspicious for a while. I can’t shake it that easily. That’s really why I came in, because it’s not just a passing thought anymore. It keeps coming back that people at work, and even some vendors, are watching what I buy and messing with the launch numbers so I look stupid.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the plot you think is happening against you as something that could realistically happen in everyday life?\n\nPatient: I guess… that’s part of what makes it hard for me, because it doesn’t feel impossible. Like, if I was saying something totally out there, maybe I could dismiss it easier. But people at work do track buys. Vendors do send numbers and reports, and launch numbers can get adjusted or entered wrong or explained in different ways. So in my head it’s like, yes, this could happen in real life.\n\nBut then it goes further for me. It’s not just, “Maybe there was a mistake.” It’s that I can’t shake the feeling that certain people at work, and even some vendors, are watching what I’m buying and messing with the launch numbers on purpose so I look stupid or incompetent. That’s the part I keep getting stuck on.\n\nAnd my supervisor has shown me reports and tried to explain that they don’t prove anyone is doing that. I can sit there and understand what he’s showing me, but it doesn’t really make the thought go away. I’ll still think maybe the report just doesn’t show it, or maybe they’re being careful about how they do it.\n\nSo… realistically, could someone mess with numbers or track buys? Yeah, I think that could happen. But I also know the way I’m holding onto it is probably more intense than other people would. Still, it feels real to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about people plotting against you would change if someone you completely trusted told you there was no plot?\n\nPatient: If it was someone I completely trusted… I mean, I think it would matter. I wouldn’t just brush it off the same way. Like if it was someone I really believed had no reason to lie to me, part of me would want to listen and calm down.\n\nBut I don’t know if it would make the belief just disappear. That’s the part that bothers me. Because even when my supervisor shows me the reports and explains that they don’t prove anyone is tracking my buys or changing the launch numbers, I can understand what he’s saying in the moment. I can follow the logic. But then later, or even while he’s talking, this other thought comes in like, okay, but what if the report just doesn’t show it? What if the vendors are involved too, or what if people are being careful so it looks normal?\n\nSo if someone I trusted said, “There’s no plot,” I think I’d probably try to believe them. I might even feel a little relieved for a bit. But I don’t think it would fully settle it for me right away, because the feeling is so stuck in my head — that people at work, and maybe vendors too, are watching what I buy and messing with the launch numbers so I look stupid. It keeps coming back even when I’m given reasons it shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your feelings of being plotted against could be related to your own stress or anxiety about work rather than an actual plot?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that, yeah. I mean… I know work has been stressful, and I know when I’m stressed I probably read into things more. So part of me can say, maybe this is my anxiety about work making everything feel more threatening than it is.\n\nBut then it doesn’t feel like just anxiety when it’s happening. What really made me come in is that I can’t shake this feeling that people at work — and even some of the vendors — are tracking what I buy and messing with the launch numbers so I look stupid. Like they’re setting it up so it looks like I don’t know what I’m doing.\n\nAnd I know my supervisor has tried to show me the reports and explain that they don’t actually prove that anyone is doing that. I can sit there and look at the reports and understand what he’s saying. But the thought doesn’t go away. It’s like my brain keeps finding another angle, like maybe the report wouldn’t show it, or maybe they changed it in a way that looks normal.\n\nSo… yes, I can admit it’s possible stress is making it worse. I don’t want to pretend I’m completely objective about it. But it still feels real to me, and that’s the problem. Even when I try to tell myself it could just be work stress, the suspicion comes right back.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, even if just for a day, where you didn't think people at work were plotting against you?\n\nPatient: Maybe for parts of a day, but not really a whole day where it’s just gone. It’s more like it quiets down if I’m busy or distracted, or if I’m not looking at the numbers right then. Like I can be doing something else and not actively thinking, “They’re plotting against me,” every single second.\n\nBut once something reminds me of it — a report, a vendor email, a launch number that doesn’t look right, or even someone at work talking in a way that seems a little off — it comes right back. And then I’m back to thinking people at work, and maybe some of the vendors too, are tracking my buys and messing with the launch numbers so I look stupid.\n\nThat’s why it’s been hard, because even when my supervisor shows me the reports and says they don’t prove anything like that is happening, I can understand what he means, but it doesn’t stay settled in my head. So I guess… no, I don’t think I’ve had a full day recently where I truly didn’t believe it at all. More like moments where I’m not focused on it, and then it comes back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about people plotting against you would change if you were presented with indisputable evidence that proved no one was plotting against you?\n\nPatient: If it was really indisputable… I mean, I want to say yes, because logically that should change it. If someone could show me something that completely proved no one at work was doing anything, no one was tracking my buys, no vendors were involved, nobody was messing with the launch numbers to make me look stupid… then I would like to think I could let it go.\n\nBut I’m being honest, part of me worries I’d still start looking for a loophole. Like, my supervisor has already shown me reports and explained that they don’t prove what I think they prove, and in the moment I can sit there and understand what he’s saying. I’m not just ignoring him. I can follow the numbers, I can hear his explanation. But then the feeling comes back anyway, like maybe the reports don’t show the whole thing, or maybe there’s something being hidden, or maybe the numbers were changed in a way that looks normal.\n\nSo if it was truly indisputable evidence, maybe it would help more than what I’ve been shown so far. I might calm down, at least for a while. But the thing that scares me is that this isn’t just a normal work concern anymore. It feels stuck, like I can’t shake it, even when I’m given reasons that should make me stop thinking it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about people plotting against you could be considered irrational or unrealistic by others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can see how other people would call it irrational or unrealistic. I mean, if I step outside of myself for a second, I know how it sounds — like I’m saying people at work and even vendors are tracking what I buy and messing with launch numbers just to make me look stupid. I get that most people would probably say, “That’s a big leap,” or “Why would they go through all that?”\n\nAnd my supervisor has tried to show me the reports, and he says they don’t prove anything like that is happening. I can sit there and understand what he’s pointing at, like the numbers don’t directly show some plot. So yeah, I know other people might think I’m being unrealistic.\n\nBut at the same time, when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel irrational. It feels like I’m noticing things other people are dismissing. That’s the hard part. I can admit it might look unreasonable from the outside, but inside my head it still feels real, and I can’t shake it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about people plotting against you would be considered delusions by a mental health professional?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, I think a mental health professional might call it that. I don’t really like saying that word, because it makes it sound like I’m just making it up or I’m completely out of touch, and that’s not how it feels to me. It feels like I’m seeing a pattern that matters.\n\nBut what really made me come in is exactly that I can’t shake it. I keep feeling like people at work, and even some of the vendors, are tracking my buys and messing with the launch numbers so I look stupid. And even when my supervisor sits down with me and shows me the reports and says, “This doesn’t prove that,” I can understand what he’s showing me, but it doesn’t make the feeling go away.\n\nSo… if a professional heard that I still believe it even after being shown reasons not to, then yeah, I can see how they might consider it a delusion. I’m not saying I fully agree with that, because part of me still feels like something is going on, but I can understand why you’d use that word.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the plot you believe is happening against you is part of a larger, organized plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s like… a huge organized thing with lots of people sitting in a room planning it out. That sounds extreme when I say it that way. But it does feel bigger than just one person messing with me, because it’s not only my coworkers in my head. It feels like people at work and even some of the vendors are involved somehow, like they’re tracking what I buy and then the launch numbers come out in a way that makes me look stupid or incompetent.\n\nThat’s part of why I can’t let it go. If it were just one weird comment or one mistake in a report, maybe I could brush it off. But it feels connected, like the buys, the vendors, the numbers, the way things get presented afterward… like there’s a pattern. And then my supervisor shows me the reports and says they don’t prove that, and I can follow what he’s saying, but it still doesn’t make the feeling go away.\n\nSo… I guess yes, in the sense that it feels coordinated to me. I’m not sure how organized or how far it goes, but it doesn’t feel random.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the plot against you is highly organized and systematic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “highly organized” exactly, because that makes it sound like there’s some formal system or like I’ve got proof of a whole chain of people doing it step by step. I don’t have that kind of proof, and I know that’s part of why this sounds… I don’t know, intense.\n\nBut it does feel systematic to me in the sense that it keeps lining up around the same thing — my buys, the vendors, the launch numbers, and then somehow it makes me look stupid or like I don’t know what I’m doing. It’s not just one random mistake that happened once and I got embarrassed. It’s more like I keep seeing the same pattern, and that’s what I can’t get out of my head.\n\nWhat really pushed me to come in is that I can’t shake it, even when my supervisor shows me the reports and says, basically, “Look, this doesn’t prove anyone is doing that.” I can sit there and understand what he’s saying, but it still feels like something is happening behind it. So… maybe not highly organized like a big operation, but coordinated enough that it feels deliberate to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like they’re setting it up so it looks like I don’t know what I’m doing.\n\nAnd I know my supervisor has tried to show me the reports and explain that they don’t actually prove that anyone is doing that. I can sit there and look at the reports and understand what he’s saying. But the thought doesn’t go away. It’s like my brain keeps finding another angle, like maybe the report wouldn’t show it, or maybe they changed it in a way that looks normal.\n\nSo… yes, I can admit it’s possible stress is making it worse. I don’t want to pretend I’m completely objective about it. But it still feels real to me, and that’s the problem. Even when I try to tell myself it could just be work stress, the suspicion comes right back.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, even if just for a day, where you didn't think people at work were plotting against you?\n\nPatient: Maybe for parts of a day, but not really a whole day where it’s just gone. It’s more like it quiets down if I’m busy or distracted, or if I’m not looking at the numbers right then. Like I can be doing something else and not actively thinking, “They’re plotting against me,” every single second.\n\nBut once something reminds me of it — a report, a vendor email, a launch number that doesn’t look right, or even someone at work talking in a way that seems a little off — it comes right back. And then I’m back to thinking people at work, and maybe some of the vendors too, are tracking my buys and messing with the launch numbers so I look stupid.\n\nThat’s why it’s been hard, because even when my supervisor shows me the reports and says they don’t prove anything like that is happening, I can understand what he means, but it doesn’t stay settled in my head. So I guess… no, I don’t think I’ve had a full day recently where I truly didn’t believe it at all. More like moments where I’m not focused on it, and then it comes back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about people plotting against you would change if you were presented with indisputable evidence that proved no one was plotting against you?\n\nPatient: If it was really indisputable… I mean, I want to say yes, because logically that should change it. If someone could show me something that completely proved no one at work was doing anything, no one was tracking my buys, no vendors were involved, nobody was messing with the launch numbers to make me look stupid… then I would like to think I could let it go.\n\nBut I’m being honest, part of me worries I’d still start looking for a loophole. Like, my supervisor has already shown me reports and explained that they don’t prove what I think they prove, and in the moment I can sit there and understand what he’s saying. I’m not just ignoring him. I can follow the numbers, I can hear his explanation. But then the feeling comes back anyway, like maybe the reports don’t show the whole thing, or maybe there’s something being hidden, or maybe the numbers were changed in a way that looks normal.\n\nSo if it was truly indisputable evidence, maybe it would help more than what I’ve been shown so far. I might calm down, at least for a while. But the thing that scares me is that this isn’t just a normal work concern anymore. It feels stuck, like I can’t shake it, even when I’m given reasons that should make me stop thinking it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about people plotting against you could be considered irrational or unrealistic by others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can see how other people would call it irrational or unrealistic. I mean, if I step outside of myself for a second, I know how it sounds — like I’m saying people at work and even vendors are tracking what I buy and messing with launch numbers just to make me look stupid. I get that most people would probably say, “That’s a big leap,” or “Why would they go through all that?”\n\nAnd my supervisor has tried to show me the reports, and he says they don’t prove anything like that is happening. I can sit there and understand what he’s pointing at, like the numbers don’t directly show some plot. So yeah, I know other people might think I’m being unrealistic.\n\nBut at the same time, when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel irrational. It feels like I’m noticing things other people are dismissing. That’s the hard part. I can admit it might look unreasonable from the outside, but inside my head it still feels real, and I can’t shake it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about people plotting against you would be considered delusions by a mental health professional?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, I think a mental health professional might call it that. I don’t really like saying that word, because it makes it sound like I’m just making it up or I’m completely out of touch, and that’s not how it feels to me. It feels like I’m seeing a pattern that matters.\n\nBut what really made me come in is exactly that I can’t shake it. I keep feeling like people at work, and even some of the vendors, are tracking my buys and messing with the launch numbers so I look stupid. And even when my supervisor sits down with me and shows me the reports and says, “This doesn’t prove that,” I can understand what he’s showing me, but it doesn’t make the feeling go away.\n\nSo… if a professional heard that I still believe it even after being shown reasons not to, then yeah, I can see how they might consider it a delusion. I’m not saying I fully agree with that, because part of me still feels like something is going on, but I can understand why you’d use that word.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the plot you believe is happening against you is part of a larger, organized plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s like… a huge organized thing with lots of people sitting in a room planning it out. That sounds extreme when I say it that way. But it does feel bigger than just one person messing with me, because it’s not only my coworkers in my head. It feels like people at work and even some of the vendors are involved somehow, like they’re tracking what I buy and then the launch numbers come out in a way that makes me look stupid or incompetent.\n\nThat’s part of why I can’t let it go. If it were just one weird comment or one mistake in a report, maybe I could brush it off. But it feels connected, like the buys, the vendors, the numbers, the way things get presented afterward… like there’s a pattern. And then my supervisor shows me the reports and says they don’t prove that, and I can follow what he’s saying, but it still doesn’t make the feeling go away.\n\nSo… I guess yes, in the sense that it feels coordinated to me. I’m not sure how organized or how far it goes, but it doesn’t feel random.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the plot against you is highly organized and systematic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “highly organized” exactly, because that makes it sound like there’s some formal system or like I’ve got proof of a whole chain of people doing it step by step. I don’t have that kind of proof, and I know that’s part of why this sounds… I don’t know, intense.\n\nBut it does feel systematic to me in the sense that it keeps lining up around the same thing — my buys, the vendors, the launch numbers, and then somehow it makes me look stupid or like I don’t know what I’m doing. It’s not just one random mistake that happened once and I got embarrassed. It’s more like I keep seeing the same pattern, and that’s what I can’t get out of my head.\n\nWhat really pushed me to come in is that I can’t shake it, even when my supervisor shows me the reports and says, basically, “Look, this doesn’t prove anyone is doing that.” I can sit there and understand what he’s saying, but it still feels like something is happening behind it. So… maybe not highly organized like a big operation, but coordinated enough that it feels deliberate to me."
  }
